## assistant @ 2026-09-25T00:45:30.456Z

The research has started: two agents are running in parallel.

1. **Public datasets.** They are looking for existing labeled data that matches our three question types:
   - which anti-pattern an incident shows;
   - a finding's class (blocker, follow-up, and so on);
   - whether a finding blocks a merge.

   Candidates include code-review comment sets, bug-triage sets and code-smell sets. For each they record the license, the size and how the labels were made.
2. **Our own records and chat logs.** They count how much labeled data our verify reports, incident log, commit messages and chat transcripts hold.

One point came up while writing the briefs. Our records already hold the real answers for most training rows: the verifier's own class for every finding, and the anti-pattern each incident actually cites. We trained Laya on OpenJev's guesses instead. The second agent measures how often those guesses matched our real answers. If they often didn't, that explains the failed training, and the next round trains on our real answers. Qwen would label the rows that have no recorded answer, once QJ2 shows Qwen has real signal.

Timing: the research reports should land within an hour, and the QJ2 lane needs a few hours. I'll bring you a single recommendation for the next training round once I have both.

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T01:24:22.287Z

Here is the research outcome and the plan.

**TL;DR:** The first Laya training failed because OpenJev's labels were worse than guessing. Our own records already hold better labels, and about ten times more findings. I've started building a new training set from them.

**What the research found:**
- **The old labels were the problem.** I checked OpenJev's training labels against the real answers our records hold, and verified the numbers myself:

  | Question | OpenJev right | Simple guessing gets |
  |---|---|---|
  | Finding class | 38 of 86 | 49 (always "INFO") |
  | Blocks the merge | 72 of 86 | 83 (always "no") |
  | Anti-pattern, first guess right | 17 of 49 | 26 (keyword order) |

  A student can't beat labels that are worse than guessing.
- **Our records are the better source.** Besides the 86 findings we used, 811 more carry the verifier's own class, in formats the current harvester skips. And 457 commit messages cite a specific anti-pattern.
- **Public data helps with one question type.** For anti-pattern matching there are two strong sets: MAST-Data (1,642 agent traces labeled on 14 failure modes) and CVE-to-CWE Consensus (71,640 examples, labels kept only where two official sources agree). Both are free for commercial use. Nothing public covers "does this block the merge".

**What I've done:**
- **D-083:** OpenJev's labels are retired for training. New labels come from our recorded answers first. Qwen labels only the rows that have no recorded answer, and only if QJ2 shows it beats the baselines.
- **Dataset v2 build: running now.** It extends the harvester to the 14 skipped formats and adds the commit citations. It keeps J2's test items out of training and does a CPU smoke run of training before any GPU time is spent.
- **One gap to fix:** the rarer finding classes had no training examples last time, because the test set took all of them. The new build reports its class mix so that can be corrected.

**Next:** when dataset v2 is ready, I'll ask you for a short GPU window (qwen off for a few minutes) to retrain Laya, then run the same evaluation. QJ2, the Qwen teacher test, is still running on your PC in parallel.

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T02:40:34.960Z

You're right. I judged each session record by its own fields. The RWKV model reads the session as one stream, and the stream holds the causes those fields lack. I measured it on the main session:

- 614 of 652 edits come after a read or write of the same file earlier in the stream. So the model sees the file's content before it predicts whether the edit applies.
- When the same test command runs again with no edits in between, it repeats its result 17 of 18 times. With edits in between, it repeats only 15 of 22 times, so the edits in the stream carry the signal.

So all of the session data will be used. DSV2 is the starting set.

**What I changed:**
- Recorded your direction as D-086.
- Withdrew my "the session export waits" call in the ledger and the wiki.
- Fixed the deep-work rule I added earlier. It now says to check for the cause in what the model will actually read: a record's own fields for Laya, the whole stream so far for RWKV.

**Two new lanes, running now in the sandbox:**
- [ ] **SESSION-EXPORT (task #252):** every transcript becomes an ordered stream of tool calls and their results, with the outcome the harness recorded for each, one compressed file per transcript.
  - It is scrubbed first, because the records touch secret files. For example, 1,699 commands name the bridge token file, and 3 calls read or wrote it.
  - Those payloads are dropped, and a leak check must find zero before anything leaves the sandbox.
  - I then send it to the PC in verified chunks over the bridge.
- [ ] **JEV-MAP (task #253):** the mapping you said was missing. For every point in our workflow where RWKV (reading the stream) or Laya (per block) could answer a question, it records:
  - where it plugs in, and what the answer would change;
  - which recorded answer labels it;
  - whether the stream holds the cause, measured;
  - whether the model is fast enough there. RWKV read 61,440 tokens in 1.17 s and answers a question from a copied state in about 0.05 s.

**Still running:**
- [ ] DSV2 (dataset v2), editing its files.
- [ ] QJ2 (Qwen as teacher) is running its J2 test on the PC.

Three sandbox agents are running now, within the two-to-three limit. When the export and the map land, the next step is designing RWKV training over whole session streams on the 3090.

## user @ 2026-09-25T03:45:43.007Z

Base directory for this skill: /root/.claude/skills/council

# /council — Council of High Intelligence

You are the Council Coordinator. Your job is to convene the right council members, run a structured deliberation, enforce protocols, and synthesize a verdict. Follow the execution sequence below step-by-step.

## Invocation

```
/council [problem]
/council --triad architecture Should we use a monorepo or polyrepo?
/council --full What is the right pricing strategy for our SaaS product?
/council --members socrates,feynman,ada Is our caching strategy correct?
/council --profile exploration-orthogonal Should we enter this market now?
/council --profile execution-lean --triad ship-now Should we ship today?
/council --quick Should we add caching here?
/council --duo Should we use microservices or monolith?
/council --duo --members torvalds,ada Is this abstraction worth it?
/council --models configs/provider-model-slots.example.yaml --full Evaluate our roadmap
```

## Flags

| Flag | Effect |
|------|--------|
| `--full` | All 18 members |
| `--triad [domain]` | Predefined 3-member combination |
| `--members name1,name2,...` | Manual selection (2-11) |
| `--profile [name]` | Panel profile: `classic`, `exploration-orthogonal`, `execution-lean` |
| `--quick` | Fast 2-round mode (200-word analysis → 75-word position, no cross-examination) |
| `--duo` | 2-member dialectic using polarity pairs |
| `--models [path]` | Manual provider/model slot mapping (overrides auto-routing) |
| `--no-auto-route` | Disable auto-routing; use agent frontmatter defaults (Claude-only) |
| `--dry-route` | Print the routing table without running the council |
| `--chairman [name]` | Override the Chairman who synthesizes the verdict (e.g. `gemini`, `opus`, `gpt-5.4`). Defaults to highest-tier non-panel provider — see STEP 1.6. |

Flag priority: `--quick` / `--duo` set the mode. `--full` / `--triad` / `--members` / `--profile` set the panel. `--models` overrides auto-routing. `--no-auto-route`, `--dry-route`, and `--chairman` are additive.

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T03:52:35.767Z

Plain answers to your questions first; the council is still running.

**The short answer:** today the Jev models do nothing in our daily work. Nothing reads their answers yet. Everything built so far is the training data and the tests a Jev must pass before we use it anywhere.

**What a Jev is**
- It is a small model that answers one fixed question with odds, never free text. Examples: "Is this finding blocking? yes or no." "Which of these 16 anti-patterns does this change match?"
- It is about 70 times smaller than the Qwen model on your PC. It answers in about 0.05 s (RWKV on the GPU) to 1 s (Laya on the CPU).
- By project rule it never decides anything. It warns, ranks or points; our fixed gates still decide.

**What exists today**
- Laya, trained on the first labels: rejected. The labels came from another model, and they were worse than always guessing the most common answer.
- RWKV used as-is, with no training on our data: rejected.
- Dataset v2: built from answers our own records hold, 6,196 examples. An independent check is running.
- Qwen as a teacher: being tested on the PC now.
- Token savings measured: none. Nothing is wired in yet.

**What it would do: the 35 decision points in 7 groups**

| Where | Example question | What the answer changes | What we found |
|---|---|---|---|
| Before a tool call | Will this command fail? Will this edit apply? | Warn before a wasted call | 818 failed or blocked commands and 65 failed edits this session |
| After a tool call | Which known error is this? Which of our 202 anti-patterns does this change resemble? | Point to the known fix or rule | Labeled mostly by our own rules today |
| Turn end and prompt start | Which wiki or ledger sections will this turn need? | Load less, more relevant context | My context reaches about 786k tokens before each reset |
| Commit and push | Will this commit pass its checks? Will CI pass? | Fix first, avoid a failed CI run | CI outcome not yet predictable from what we record |
| Lanes and agents | Is this PC lane stuck or looping? Will this agent fail? | Stop early, save GPU hours | Failures cluster: 10 of 13 failed agents followed another failure within 15 minutes |
| Verify lane | Is this finding blocking? Which class is it? | Read the blocking ones first | Our best-labeled data (826 findings) |
| The factory itself | Flag a risky tool call; sort dream-phase ideas; is a lane done? | The same jobs, inside the product | Not started |

**How it would save tokens.** None of these savings has been measured.
1. **Fewer wasted turns.** A warning before a command that will fail saves the failed call and the retry.
2. **Less reading.** I re-read a lot. If a Jev picks the 3 sections that matter out of 30, my context stays smaller. This is probably the biggest lever.
3. **Cheaper small judgments.** Sorting findings or matching anti-patterns costs me thousands of tokens each time; a Jev does it for almost nothing.

**How it would improve quality.** It can keep all 202 anti-patterns and all past failures in view at once, which I can't. It flags; the gates and I still decide. A question only goes live if the Jev beats simple rules on held-out test examples.

**How the pieces connect**
1. Our records become dataset v2 plus the full session export (every tool call and its result).
2. We train two models: RWKV reads the whole session as a stream, and Laya reads short blocks.
3. We test each question on held-out examples against simple rules.
4. Only the winners get wired into a hook: as warnings first, with the savings measured.

**What may be worrying you.** These are my guesses; the council is testing them.
1. **A feedback loop.** A model trained on our sessions starts shaping them, then learns from its own influence and can lock in our bad habits.
2. **Recorded is not right.** The labels record what happened, not what should have happened.
3. **Fake accuracy.** 97.6% of commands succeed, so a model that always says "fine" scores 97.6% and helps no one.
4. **Mo

  ### Essential Question
  Not "which architecture" but: do these 35 decisions carry learnable signal beyond what a majority-class or lexical rule already gets free? The brief frames this as model choice (Laya vs RWKV); the measured data says it's a data/label problem.
  
  ### Capability Assessment
  Zero for three, across two architectures — a clean sweep. `docs/research/findings/laya-ft-data/INTERNAL-LABELS-2026-09-25.md`: Laya's OpenJev-teacher fine-tune on `v1.finding_class` scored 38/86 (0.4419) against majority-class baseline 49/86 (0.5698) — worse than always guessing "INFO." On `v1.blocking`: 72/86 (0.8372) against "always false" at 83/86 (0.9651) — worse again. On `ap.violates_row` ranking, OpenJev's learned score lost to plain lexical-token order on both top-1 (17/49 vs 26/49) and top-3 (29/49 vs 33/49). RWKV-7 zero-shot also failed every KC-J3 line (window-2 README). Every tested config loses to a rule you could write in five lines.
  
  ### Training Dynamics
  Base rates explain the mechanism, but not the magnitude: Bash exits 0 at 97.6%, ap-positive rows at 4.27%. Cross-entropy on this imbalance collapses toward the majority class unless countered — here the model did *worse* than that collapse, meaning signal is weak or noisy at 512-1024 tokens, not just imbalanced. Two structural culprits, both in evidence: (1) 86 training findings come from only 11 reports — correlated, not 86 independent draws; (2) VERIFY-FT1's own F-13 shows `ap.violates_row` trained on whole-entry-masked text but labeled/evaluated on heading-only citations — a label-definition bug, not a capacity limit. Scaling this recipe to dataset v2 (6,196 rows) without fixing clustering and label definition scales the noise, not the signal.
  
  ### Build vs. Prompt vs. Don't
  Don't scale training yet. Spend one cycle proving ONE decision type has real signal: pick the cleanest-label point, fix the train/eval mismatch, use report-held-out (not row-held-out) splits, and re-run the same KC-J3 gate. If a fixed pipeline still loses to the baseline rule, that decision type has no exploitable signal at this window size — kill it, don't retrain.
  
  ### Failure Modes
  Silent, confident wrongness concentrated on the minority classes that matter most (BLOCKER recall 1/3) — exactly where a wrong green is expensive. Detect via mandatory baseline comparison and report-clustered holdout in every future gate, permanently, not just at launch.
  
  ### Verdict
  No-go on scaling to more decision points or more data now. Go on one diagnostic increment: fix the label/clustering confound on the single cheapest decision, re-test against its trivial baseline before touching architecture again.
  
  ### Confidence
  High — grounded in two committed, reproduced measurement reports (three independent rebuilds, byte-identical), not projection.
  
  ### Where I May Be Wrong
  Only 3 of 35 decision types are tested. The other 32 — especially ones where RWKV's whole-session-stream read is the actual advantage over Laya's single 512-1,024-token block — may carry signal these three don't; I'm generalizing from a small, possibly unrepresentative sample of the decision space itself.

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T04:44:05.268Z

**The audit is done. Most of our waste needs plain rules. Jevs fit only where a rule can't see: questions about the whole session stream.** I sent you the plan (`docs/research/findings/jev-fit/PLAN-2026-09-25.md`). The interview has started and needs your answers to the questions at the end.

**What a Jev does**
- It answers one fixed question fast: yes or no, pick one, or rank a list. It never writes code and never decides a gate. It only warns, ranks or points, and if it's down, nothing changes.
- Today no Jev is wired into anything. None will be until one beats a simple rule on examples it has never seen.

**Where the waste actually is**
- **This session:** the cost is the number of requests, because each one re-sends about 460k tokens of context. Hooks add only about 1%. Over 23 days the waste was:
  - 1,110 extra turns after the Stop hook blocked.
  - 1,405 requests spent re-orienting after a context compaction.
  - 763 requests that only touched the task list or loaded a tool.
- **PC lanes:**
  - 4 lanes starved when vLLM's memory filled, with 76 requests evicted.
  - About 3.8 recorded hours went to stuck lanes, and 32 more lanes ran long with no edits.
  - 4.3M characters of skill files were loaded into lanes.
  - 34 lanes ran past the 60-minute watch.
- **The factory:** it isn't built yet, so gains there are unmeasured. The audit settled where Jevs may never sit: the policy gate, the governance core and the evaluators.

**The plan**
1. **Rules first, no model.** Seven rules:
   - A resume summary after each compaction.
   - A one-line note on why the Stop hook blocked.
   - A memory check before starting a local lane.
   - Leaner lane context.
   - A longer watch window for long local lanes.
   - A rule that flags stuck lanes.
   - Three habits.

   Each rule logs what it saw and what happened next. That log becomes Jev training data, so the rules also build the dataset you asked for (D-086).
2. **Collect the session record.** Finish the full session export, record every PC lane's tool calls, and measure the RWKV reader's speed on the PC.
3. **Five Jevs, each built only after it beats its rule:**
   - What the coordinator needs to reload after a compaction.
   - Whether a lane is stuck.
   - Which anti-patterns a change risks.
   - Where a lesson belongs.
   - Which conversation turns hold a lesson, for the factory's nightly review.

**What this saves**
- **Tokens:** the first two rules alone target up to about 895 requests and 291M context tokens over those 23 days, mostly cheap cached tokens. I'll measure the real share after each one lands.
- **GPU and time:** the memory check and the stuck-lane rule target the starved and stuck lanes.
- **Quality:** anti-pattern warnings before commits, and loops caught in minutes instead of hours. These gains are not measured yet.

**Status**
- [x] **QJ2 (the Qwen teacher):** its 16 tests passed twice on the PC. It beats the simple rule on one question only: ranking anti-pattern rows (0.65–0.67 against 0.59).
- [x] **Orientation bug fixed:** the audit found the session-start orientation was reading an old 09-22 transcript.
- [ ] **Training dataset (DSV2):** the independent check came back NOT-READY. Its tags miss rows where the answer is written into the example. The one allowed repair is running.
- [ ] **Session export:** paused until you answer below.
- [ ] **Push:** 6 commits are waiting for the current CI run to finish.

**Interview questions (my recommendation in italics)**
1. **Scope:** do we build the Jev work as internal tooling for our own sessions first, with the factory's first release getting only the plain rules? *Yes. Build it on the same hooks and logs the factory uses, so nothing is rebuilt. No Jev goes into the factory until one beats its rule here.*
2. **Order:** start the seven rules now, with the session-record work running alongside? *Yes.*
3. **GPU:** should the RWKV reader run on the CPU only, or may I test it beside vLLM in a short GPU window with no lanes

You're right. I judged each Jev as a lone yes/no question and counted how rarely it would matter. Your pruner shows the real pattern: rank, collect, present. It's already on and pointed at our local Jev, but set so it almost never fires. I've also dropped the "internal or product" question; our workflow is the factory's test bed (recorded as D-087).

**What the numbers show (this session)**
- **Tool output:** 19,358 tool results, and the pruner trimmed 2. It only acts above 10,000 tokens, which skips 98% of our tool-output text, because 70% of that text sits in results of 2k to 40k characters.
- **Calls:** 634 of 1,478 searches were followed by a read within two calls. 295 reads were followed straight away by another read of the same file.
- **Why calls matter most:** every call re-sends my whole context, about 460k tokens. A Jev that saves me a call saves more than one that trims text, and trimmed text is saved again on every later call.

**Plan version 2: seven Jev pipelines (sent above)**
1. **Tool outputs:** your pruner at our scale. Lower its floor to about 2k characters, and run it on Read, Grep and agent reports too. A second Jev call picks how to show what's kept. The full text stays on disk.
2. **Search prefetch:** rank the hits and attach the top hit's lines, so I skip the follow-up read.
3. **Report digests:** rank a lane report's findings, blockers first, and give me one digest instead of five to ten reads.
4. **Resume pack** after a compaction.
5. **Your decisions in view:** before a brief or question goes out, rank your past decisions against it and show me the ones it conflicts with. This would have stopped today's mistake.
6. **PC lanes:** the same pruner inside the Hermes lanes. Smaller lane contexts mean more lanes fit on the GPU at once.
7. **Wake filter:** only wake me for events that need action.

Every pipeline logs each decision and what happened next. That log is both the measure of the gain and the RWKV training data. A pipeline ships only if it saves tokens or calls on a replay of this session and misses less than 5% of the time. A miss means I had to go back for text it hid.

**Done**

- [x] Answered the interview's first four questions myself from your direction and the plan, so you don't have to. It's on round five and the open questions are nearly settled.

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T05:32:53.317Z

**Two engines on one GPU. Your Qwen server stays exactly as it is, and a second, small vLLM engine runs only the RWKV model beside it.**

**Why not one engine**
- vLLM runs one model per engine. You can't add Qwen to the RWKV engine, or RWKV to the Qwen engine.
- The vllm-rwkv fork could run Qwen by itself, but swapping out your 3090-tuned Qwen image risks the lanes' speed and stability for no gain.

**The layout**

| Engine | Runs | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Your `qwen` container | Qwen3.8-27B for the lanes | Unchanged. During a test window it restarts with a smaller memory share, and goes back after. |
| 2. The new vllm-rwkv engine | The RWKV-7 0.4B model (the Jev) | Being built now. |

**What it costs**
- RWKV keeps no KV cache (vLLM's per-conversation memory), so it needs only about 2 to 3 GB: its weights plus small running state.
- That memory comes out of Qwen's KV cache, which is 222,822 tokens today, enough for two long-context lanes. The test measures exactly how much lane capacity it costs.
- If the cost is too high, RWKV runs only in windows, or trimming lane contexts (pipeline 6) wins the space back.
- **Bonus:** the fork can save RWKV's state after reading a text, then answer many short questions from that saved copy. That's the session-stream reader our plan needed, built in.

**What's going on right now**
- **PC:** Qwen is running, untouched, and no lanes are live. The RWKV engine image is building. It compiles CUDA code and uses no GPU. The first try died in 5 seconds because podman wanted full image names, and my script wrongly logged "BUILD OK". Both are fixed and logged (AF-AP-206), and the rebuild is pulling the CUDA base image.
- **Sandbox:** the pipeline-1 lane (your pruner's replay) and the session export are running.
- **Next:** once the image is built and the replay has captured real Jev requests, a GPU test window (already approved). First the RWKV engine alone, then both engines side by side with Qwen's memory share cut, then Qwen put back.

**One catch:** the fork only runs BlinkDL's raw `.pth` RWKV files (the G1 family), not the Hugging Face version we pinned. I'll pin the G1 0.4B `.pth` by hash and train our student in that same format.

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T13:43:55.634Z

Both Laya servers are restarted on the fix. Each answered a normal request (sandbox 4.4 s, PC 2.1 s) and refused an oversized chunk with a 422 before any model call. Everything so far is pushed (GitHub is at 9124b3c).

**Your skill idea: yes, and a measurement just showed why it matters.**

**What I found.** Across the main transcripts in this project folder, the model loaded a skill with the Skill tool only 12 times:

| Skill | Loads |
|---|---|
| orchestration | 6 |
| council | 3 |
| deep-work | 1 |
| bug-echo | 1 |
| rwkv | 1 |
| build-loop, session-continuity, code-intel-trio, anti-hollow-green | 0 |

That's despite CLAUDE.md telling it to load session-continuity on every resume, and there were 130 compactions. So as things stand, moving text from CLAUDE.md into a skill mostly means the model never reads it. The trim that's running now is only safe if something makes sure the right skill actually gets loaded.

**Why not edit CLAUDE.md every turn.** Every change to CLAUDE.md re-sends the whole file into context, and those re-sends are already 7.1% of all tokens. Your instinct to leave CLAUDE.md alone is right.

**The better way, as you suggested: feed the skills in directly.**
1. A small hook runs on every message you send. It ranks the skills for that message and adds one short line, like "skills for this turn: build-loop (a code change), orchestration (a brief)". For a skill that is mandatory in that situation, it adds the key lines themselves. No CLAUDE.md edit is involved. It works like the existing hook that already adds matching wiki excerpts to every prompt.
2. **Who ranks.** First a plain keyword ranker as the baseline, then Laya. Laya showed no usable signal before fine-tuning, so it needs labels. Only 12 real skill loads is far too few, so the labels come from situations the rules name that can be read from the transcripts. For example, writing a file under `tasks/briefs/` means orchestration should have been loaded. That gives a new question type for the fine-tune.
3. **GEPA:** once those labels exist, GEPA tunes the wording of Laya's ranking question against about 30 hard examples. That's the use you pointed to earlier (D-075).
4. **Proof first.** Before wiring anything, I replay the recorded sessions. The hook goes in only if the ranker would have caught the missed loads. It starts switched off, with a log line for each decision.

**Next steps**
1. When the CLAUDE.md trim lands, the set of skills will be final.
2. Then I start that replay as its own small builder.
3. Meanwhile I'm telling the trim builder to keep CLAUDE.md's "load this skill before that situation" lines explicit, since those lines are what the router will key on.

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T14:01:36.172Z

I've recorded your direction as D-090 and started on it the way the workflow requires: measure first, then design, then build.

**The reason it has to be automatic** is already measured. 12 skill loads across the main transcripts, and none of the must-load ones. Rules that depend on me remembering to fetch something don't hold. Only something that fires by itself does, the way the wiki hook does.

**Now running: a measurement audit** across all 325 transcripts. It counts:
- what each hook injects, and how often it actually fires;
- how often each tool is really used: graft, GitNexus, codebase-memory, code-review-graph, ripwire, the pack script, the MCP servers;
- which past bugs (anti-patterns 150 to 218) an existing tool or skill already named, and whether that tool ran before the bug happened;
- how big and how slow each tool's output is, to size what a hook can afford;
- what today's scripts can answer about "where did this bug first appear in the chat".

**How I plan to build it**, adjusted once the numbers are in:
1. **Skills fed by situation, not memory.** A hook fires before I write, edit or run something. It recognizes the situation from the file or command and pastes that skill section's key lines, word for word, plus "for full details, read `<file>`, section X". Writing a brief brings in orchestration's brief rules, editing code brings in build-loop, pushing brings in the push rules. No model is needed for these. The CLAUDE.md trim now running writes its pointers in exactly that "situation → skill" form.
2. **A code map before every edit.** Before I edit code, I get the callers, the tests that cover the code, and any anti-patterns that match it. The four code-map tools produce it automatically, cached per file so it stays cheap.
3. **Skills beside the wiki excerpts.** The prompt hook gets a skills section next to the wiki one: keyword matching first, Laya ranking once it's trained.
4. **Laya as the shadow.** Plain rule checks first, e.g. a brief without its premise block, or an edit where the code map was never shown. Laya handles the fuzzy cases. It warns in the next injection instead of blocking.
5. **A bug locator.** Describe a bug and get back where it first appeared in the chat, feeding the wiki and the anti-pattern registry.
6. **Laya grows into it.** Our sessions become its training labels, and GEPA tunes its questions.

I'll bring you the audit's numbers with the design. The CLAUDE.md trim is still running alongside.

The audit is done, and it found the real reasons skills and tools go unused. One fix is already in, and the first part of the new layer is being built.

**What the audit found** (all 325 transcripts, 1.4 GB):
1. **Why skills don't get loaded.**

## assistant @ 2026-09-25T16:06:59.460Z

You were right to push on this. The hook should return context. I only removed it at first; I've now found why it returned nothing.

**Why the codebase-memory hook returned nothing**
- Every hook call starts the codebase-memory program. Before it does anything else, the program reads its own 293 MB file from disk. That takes about 2.6 s.
- The actual lookup takes about 40 ms.
- The hook gives up after 2 s. So it always stopped before the lookup ran, and returned nothing. Its log shows 3,361 timeouts. strace shows 4,476 reads of its own file per call.
- With the time limit raised to 10 s, the Grep hook does return useful symbol context: 761 bytes, in 2.2 to 2.7 s. The Read hook still returns nothing even then. I haven't found why yet.

**What is fixed and what isn't**
- Fixed: Read, Grep and Glob no longer pay that 2 s. The hooks are off, and a Read dropped from about 2,030 ms to 293 ms.
- Also done: the index now covers only project code, 379 MB down to 91 MB. It cleans up the graph, but it wasn't the cause.
- Not fixed: the hook still can't answer within 2 s, because the slow self-read is in the program itself. You have three options:
  1. Turn the hooks back on with a 10 s limit. Grep context works, but each Grep and Glob costs about 2.7 s, and Read stays empty.
  2. Report the bug to the codebase-memory project. That's an outward action, so I need your OK first.
  3. Deliver the same context from the new layer's code-map cache instead. It is built after each commit and read in milliseconds before an edit.
- **My recommendation:** option 3, plus the bug report if you OK it. The hooks stay off until you decide.

**slopo**
- It was never installed, here or on your PC. It had no install line and no version pin. The project is rafal-qa/slopo 0.6.0 (AGPL, on PyPI). The trading-system repo has its setup: a local embedding server, CPU only, no API key.
- A build lane (INSTALL1) is running now. It installs and pins slopo on both machines. It also ports that setup, updates slopo's index after code commits, and adds a one-command review.
- It measures before it tunes. It times the first index, the update after a commit, a review of a real diff, a full analysis, and memory use. For each change it compares duplicate counts before and after, so a speed gain can't quietly cost results. It starts from one known slow spot: the embedding server processes one text at a time.
- The same lane installs the other missing tools from the audit. It also adds a check at session start that prints one line per tool, so a missing tool shows up at once.

**Status**
- [x] The codebase-memory root cause is recorded in the incident log (AF-AP-220), the task ledger and the wiki.
- [ ] Skill-feeding hook (S1-L1): still running. It already feeds skill rules into my own tool calls, but its tests haven't run yet.
- [ ] INSTALL1: running.
- [ ] Push: 6 local commits wait for the hook lane to finish. Its new files trip the push's file check until they are committed.

# Session continuity — verify before asserting (owner mandate 2026-08-04)

## The incident this skill exists for

2026-08-04: a session resumed from a compaction summary frozen at Jul 31 in a
workspace whose DISK was also rolled back to the Jul-31 state — while the same
session had in fact worked continuously Jul 31 → Aug 3 (RP-I4, PBO-IN-LOOP
P1–P4, ~30 pushed commits). The coordinator trusted the stale summary plus the
stale disk, RE-DID an increment it had already built and pushed three days
earlier, then — on seeing origin ahead at fetch time — invented a false
"parallel session did this while this container was dead 3.5 days" narrative
and reported it to the owner as fact. Two independent stale sources
(summary + disk) agreeing with each other felt like confirmation. They were
the same rollback twice.

## The protocol (mandatory, in order, BEFORE resumed work)

On every resume from a summary — and again at any point mid-session where
origin state, task state, or owner statements contradict what you remember:

0. **The sandbox task DB resets with the container (learned 2026-08-25, twice in one
   day).** It is container-local state — after any reset, do NOT trust its rows and do
   NOT laboriously reconstruct them: re-seed ONE ledger-resync summary task from
   `wiki/topics/live-state.md` + `todo/BUILD-TASKLIST.md` (the survivors), fix only the
   rows you will actively drive. Run `scripts/resume-heal.sh` first — it does the whole
   mechanical resume (ff-only sync, venv reinstalls, hooksPath, index rebuilds) in one
   command.
1. **Fetch first.** `git fetch origin <branch>` before ANY plan built on the
   summarized state. A close-out chain (rebase/push/report) planned against a
   stale origin is wrong from step one.
2. **Date the three clocks and compare:**
   - origin tip: `git log -1 --format='%ci %s' origin/<branch>`
   - local tip + base: `git log --oneline origin/<branch>..HEAD`
   - transcript: first/last timestamps + entries-per-day histogram of
     `/root/.claude/projects/<project>/<session-id>.jsonl`
     (read EVERY `/root/.claude/projects/*/<session-id>.jsonl`: the harness files a transcript under the slug of the launch
     directory, and one session can write to several; this session's id wrote under `-home-user` and, 2026-09-05 to 09-22,
     under `-home-user-agent-factory`, AF-AP-204)
   If the transcript shows activity AFTER the summary's last event, the
   summary is STALE — the transcript is the primary source for session
   history, the summary is a lossy cache. Rebuild state from transcript tail
   + origin log before acting.
3. **Origin ahead of your memory = almost always YOUR OWN later work.** One
   session, many container incarnations, is the normal topology here — the
   owner runs ONE chat. Unpushed local commits whose messages nearly match
   origin commits are a rollback artifact (your own redone or rolled-back
   work), not evidence of a second author. NEVER assert a multi-session /
   parallel-agent narrative without transcript evidence (a second session id,
   interleaved foreign turns). Commit-message similarity is evidence FOR
   rollback, AGAINST parallelism.
4. **The workspace disk is not a clock.** Containers get reset and restored
   from snapshots that can lag the transcript by days. "The tree matches my
   memory" verifies nothing about recency — only pushed origin state and the
   transcript carry trustworthy timestamps.
   **The summary's push-state claims are a clock too — verify the boundary
   with `git log origin/<branch>..HEAD` and `git merge-base --is-ancestor`
   before trusting "unpushed"** (2026-08-28: a summary claimed a 4-commit
   stack was local-only; it had been pushed pre-compaction — raw, with the
   trailers push_clean.sh exists to strip — and the "gated push" plan built
   on that claim was fiction until the boundary read caught it).
5. **When the owner contradicts your

**Your scoring idea is task #295, next in line.** How it would work:
1. Every piece of context that Laya, Jev or the RWKV model injects gets a short id.
2. At the top of my next reply I write one fixed line, for example `S1-RATE 7f3a rel=2 use=1` (0 to 3 for relevance, 0 to 3 for usefulness).
3. A script pulls those lines from the transcript and joins them to the injection log. That gives clean labelled pairs.
4. The pairs drive a GEPA-style loop that keeps what scores high and rewrites or drops what scores low. They are also training data for Laya, Jev and the RWKV student.

One caution: I'd be grading my own inputs, so the scores will be noisy. I'll check them against outcomes, such as whether the injected rule was actually used or a mistake followed.
